The sine-Gordon equation in light-cone coordinates on the half-lines revisited: a Riemann–Hilbert approach
Authors: Iryna Karpenko
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In this work, we study the initial-boundary value (IBV) problems for the sine-Gordon (sG) equation in the light-cone coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{xt}=\sin u$$\end{document}uxt=sinu in the quarter-planes x> 0, t > 0x>0,t>0 and x < 0, t > 0x<0,t>0, assuming a suitable decay as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞ or as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}x→-∞. Employing the Riemann–Hilbert (RH) problem framework, we demonstrate that these two IBV problems differ significantly with respect to the boundary data required for well-posedness. Specifically, the solution of the “right problem” (x≥ 0x≥0) is uniquely determined by the initial data u(x, 0), x≥ 0x≥0 alone, whereas for the “left problem” (x≤ 0x≤0), the boundary data u(0, t) have to be prescribed in addition to the initial data in order to obtain a well-posed problem. The latter problem is solved using the unified transform method (also known as the Fokas method).

Introduction

The sine-Gordon (sG) equation is one of the classical integrable nonlinear equations and has been extensively studied. In the laboratory coordinates (y,τ )(y,τ) it takes the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_{yy}-u_{\tau \tau }+\sin u=0. $$\end{document}uyy-uττ+sinu=0.The sG equation arises in differential geometry in the theory of surfaces of constant negative curvature and appears in several areas of physics, including the theory of crystal dislocations and the dynamics of Josephson junctions (see, e.g., [1–4]).

Introducing the light-cone variables\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ x=\frac{1}{2}(y+\tau ),\quad t=\frac{1}{2}(y-\tau ) $$\end{document}x=12(y+τ),t=12(y-τ)the equation transforms into1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{xt} = \sin u. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uxt=sinu.In these variables, x and t correspond to the characteristic directions of a hyperbolic equation.

The Cauchy problem on the whole line for the sG equation was solved using the inverse scattering transform by Ablowitz, Kaup, Newell, and Segur (see [5, 6]). This method reveals the rich structure of the sG equation, including the existence of localized solutions such as kinks and breathers, which correspond to discrete eigenvalues of the associated spectral problem (see [6]).

The analysis becomes considerably more complicated when the sG equation is posed in domains with boundaries, such as quarter-planes and semi-strips. To address such problems, Fokas introduced a general and systematic framework known as the unified transform method (UTM), widely referred to as the Fokas method (see [7]), which has subsequently been developed and extended by many authors [8–13]. The method is based on the simultaneous spectral analysis of both equations of the associated Lax pair.

In [14], Fokas and Its studied the sG equation in laboratory coordinates in the quarter-plane with prescribed initial data and boundary values (see also [7, 15]). In a later work [16], the equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{xt}=-\sin u$$\end{document}uxt=-sinu was considered on a finite spatial interval (0, l). In both formulations, the initial profile and the boundary values are assumed to be prescribed as part of the data of the problem.

An inverse spectral approach was used by Sakhnovich, who investigated the sG equation in semi-strip domains using the Weyl function associated with an auxiliary Dirac-type system arising from the Lax pair (see [17, 18]). In particular, this approach makes it possible to study the evolution of the corresponding spectral data and to describe situations in which solutions become unbounded in the quarter-plane. In [19], the Dirichlet problem on the half-line is considered with prescribed initial data u(x, 0) and boundary data u(0, t). These data are required to satisfy appropriate compatibility conditions, and the solution is reconstructed from the corresponding scattering data.

A different inverse spectral approach to the Dirichlet problem on the half-line was proposed by Leon [20]. In this work, the sG equation in light-cone coordinates (in the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{xt}=-\sin u$$\end{document}uxt=-sinu, which can be related to (1.1) by the change of variable x↦ -xx↦-x) is analyzed using an alternative Lax pair associated with a Schrödinger-type spectral problem on the half-line.

Despite the extensive literature on initial-boundary value (IBV) problems for the sine-Gordon equation, several questions concerning the well-posedness of these problems have remained open, particularly regarding the decay conditions as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|x|\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}|x|→∞. In the present paper, we address these questions by studying two initial-boundary value problems for the sG equation in the light-cone coordinates (1.1), posed on the half-lines x≥ 0x≥0 (Problem I) and x≤ 0x≤0 (Problem II), building on the Fokas method for half-line problems.

We consider the following IBV problem1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&u_{xt} = \sin u,\quad x\le 0,\quad 0\le t<T<\infty ,\\&u(x,0) = u_0(x), \quad x \le 0,\\&u(0,t)=v_0(t),\qquad t\in [0,T] \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uxt=sinu,x≤0,0≤t<T<∞,u(x,0)=u0(x),x≤0,u(0,t)=v0(t),t∈[0,T]and seek solutions u(x, t) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^1([0,T],\tilde{H}^{1,2}((-\infty ,0)))$$\end{document}u∈C1([0,T],H~1,2((-∞,0))).

The conditions imposed on the behavior of solutions of the IBV problems at the spatial infinity affect the well-posedness of half-line problems crucially. A striking illustration is provided by Sakhnovich’s treatment of the sine-Gordon equation on the quarter-plane x≥ 0x≥0, t≥ 0t≥0 [18]. Under suitable assumptions on the boundary data, assuming also that the spatial derivative of the solution is bounded in the whole quarter-plane, the boundary data determine uniquely the initial Weyl function and thus the initial values; see [18, Corollary 4.10]. In particular, zero boundary data force the initial Weyl function, and therefore the corresponding initial data, to be trivial; see [18, Example 4.11].

The outline of the paper is as follows.

In Sect. 2, we present the basic tools for our analysis derived from the Lax pair representation of the sG equation, namely the Jost solutions (“eigenfunctions”) and the associated spectral functions (scattering coefficients).

In Sect. 3, we discuss the compatibility conditions for the initial and boundary values.

In Sect. 4, we describe the inverse spectral problems for the initial and boundary values in terms of solutions of the associated RH problems.

In Sect. 5, the master RH problems are constructed, whose solutions provide the solutions to Problem I and Problem II.

In Sect. 6, we show how a local solution of the sG equation can be constructed from the solution of a RH problem parametrized by x and t.

In Sect. 7, the differentiability of the solution of the RH problem is discussed.

In Sect. 8, we address Problem I, for which we find that the initial data alone uniquely determine its solution.

In Sect. 9, we focus on Problem II, where we show that in addition to the initial data, the boundary value u(0, t) should be prescribed to uniquely determine the solution.

The main results of the paper are formulated in Theorems 8.1, 8.3, 8.8, 9.1, and 9.3.

Eigenfunctions and spectral functions

The sine-Gordon equation (1.1) is the compatibility condition for the Lax pair 2.1a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Phi }_x&=U\tilde{\Phi }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ~x=UΦ~,2.1b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Phi }_t&=V\tilde{\Phi }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ~t=VΦ~, where the coefficients U≡ U(x,t,k)U≡U(x,t,k) and V≡ V(x,t,k)V≡V(x,t,k) are defined by 2.2a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U&=-\textrm{i}k \sigma _3+\frac{u_x}{2}\begin{pmatrix} 0& 1\\ -1& 0 \end{pmatrix},\end{aligned}$$\end{document}U=-ikσ3+ux201-10,2.2b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V&=-\frac{1}{4\textrm{i}k}\begin{pmatrix} \cos u& -\sin u\\ -\sin u& -\cos u \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V=-14ikcosu-sinu-sinu-cosu.

The first step in the spectral analysis is to investigate the analyticity properties, in the complex spectral parameter k, of the eigenfunctions associated with the Lax pair. Studying their analyticity, symmetry, and boundedness in appropriate regions of the complex k-plane leads to precise relations among the different eigenfunctions. These relations can be formulated as a matrix Riemann–Hilbert factorization problem in the spectral variable.

Moreover, the coefficient matrices U and V in (2.2) are traceless. Consequently, for any matrix solution Φ (x,t,k)Φ(x,t,k) of the Lax pair (2.1) (constructed from two linearly independent vector solutions), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _x \det \Phi = 0, \qquad \partial _t \det \Phi = 0. $$\end{document}∂xdetΦ=0,∂tdetΦ=0.Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det \Phi $$\end{document}detΦ is independent of both x and t, and therefore is constant throughout the domain.

Problem I

Assume that we are given a solution u(x, t) of the Problem I (1.2) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(\cdot , t)\in \tilde{H}^{1,2}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}u(·,t)∈H~1,2(0,∞) for all t∈ [0,T]t∈[0,T]. We study the analytic properties of the associated eigenfunctions of the Lax pair in the complex spectral plane and derive relations among them that can be formulated as a factorization Riemann–Hilbert problem.

Eigenfunctions near k=∞ k=∞

To have good control as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, we rewrite the Lax pair (2.1) as 2.3a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Phi }_x+\textrm{i}k \sigma _3\tilde{\Phi }&=U_\infty \tilde{\Phi },\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ~x+ikσ3Φ~=U∞Φ~,2.3b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Phi }_t+\frac{1}{4\textrm{i}k}\sigma _3\tilde{\Phi }&=V_\infty \tilde{\Phi }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ~t+14ikσ3Φ~=V∞Φ~, where the coefficients U≡ U(x,t,k)U≡U(x,t,k) and V≡ V(x,t,k)V≡V(x,t,k) are defined by 2.4a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U_\infty&=\frac{u_x}{2}\begin{pmatrix} 0& 1\\ -1& 0 \end{pmatrix},\end{aligned}$$\end{document}U∞=ux201-10,2.4b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V_\infty&=-\frac{1}{4\textrm{i}k}\begin{pmatrix} \cos u-1& -\sin u\\ -\sin u& -\cos u+1 \end{pmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V∞=-14ikcosu-1-sinu-sinu-cosu+1.

We construct the solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Phi }_{ j}$$\end{document}Φ~j, j=1,2,3j=1,2,3, of Eq. Remark 2.1

Remark 2.2

Compatibility of initial and boundary values

In this section, we discuss the relations among the spectral functions.

Inverse spectral mappings

In this section, the inverse spectral mappings are formulated through the solutions of associated Riemann–Hilbert problems, with jump matrices determined by the corresponding spectral functions.

Let ε >0ϵ>0 be sufficiently small so that all zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{a}(k)$$\end{document}a~(k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{a}^*(k)$$\end{document}a~∗(k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{A}(k)$$\end{document}A~(k), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{A}^*(k)$$\end{document}A~∗(k) appearing in the denominators below lie outside the disks \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{|k|\le \epsilon \}$$\end{document}{|k|≤ϵ}.

Problem I

The inverse x-spectral mapping

The inverse x-spectral mapping\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \{ a(k),b(k) \}\longrightarrow \{ u_0(x) \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{a(k),b(k)}⟶{u0(x)}is based on solving a Riemann–Hilbert problem whose jump matrix is built from the given spectral functions a and b. The construction of this problem is suggested by the properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{2}(x,0,k)$$\end{document}Φ2(x,0,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{3}(x,0,k)$$\end{document}Φ3(x,0,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{02}(x,0,k)$$\end{document}Ψ02(x,0,k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{03}(x,0,k)$$\end{document}Ψ03(x,0,k) described in the direct spectral analysis.

Proof

First, consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {C}^+$$\end{document}k∈C+. u_{0x}^2(y)\textrm{d}y\\ u_{0x}(x) \end{pmatrix}+O(\frac{1}{k^2}). $$\end{document}Φ2(1)(x,0,k)=10+14ik12∫0xu0x2(y)dyu0x(x)+O(1k2).Similarly, expanding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Proof

(2) The conditions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M^{(x)}$$\end{document}M(x) ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det M^{(x)}$$\end{document}detM(x) has neither a jump across \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\cup \{|k|=\epsilon \}$$\end{document}R∪{|k|=ϵ} no singularities at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_j$$\end{document}kj. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det M^{(x)}$$\end{document}detM(x) tends to 1 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, Liouville’s theorem implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det M^{(x)}\equiv 1$$\end{document}detM(x)≡1.

(1) The uniqueness of the solution follows by applying the Liouville-type arguments to the ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M_1^{(x)}( M_2^{(x)})^{-1}$$\end{document}M1(x)(M2(x))-1 of two potential solutions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M_1^{(x)}$$\end{document}M1(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M_2^{(x)}$$\end{document}M2(x).

(3) The symmetries are inherited from the corresponding symmetries of the jump matrix and the uniqueness of the solution.

□ □

Remark 4.3

The inverse t-spectral mapping

The inverse t-spectral mapping\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \{ A(k), B(k) \}\longrightarrow \{ v_0(t) \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}{A(k),B(k)}⟶{v0(t)}can be expressed in terms of the solution of a Riemann–Hilbert problem with a jump matrix determined by A and B. This RH problem is constructed using the relations among the eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{ 1}(0,t,k)$$\end{document}Φ1(0,t,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{ 2}(0,t, k)$$\end{document}Φ2(0,t,k), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{01}(0,t, k)$$\end{document}Ψ01(0,t,k), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{02}(0,t, k)$$\end{document}Ψ02(0,t,k) arising in the direct spectral analysis.

Notice that having A(k) and B(k), we can obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _i^0$$\end{document}κi0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _i^T$$\end{document}κiT, i=1,2i=1,2, and determine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{A}( k)$$\end{document}A~(k) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{B}(k)$$\end{document}B~(k) (see (2.50) and (2.34)).

Remark 4.6

We could also define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sin \tfrac{v_0(t)}{2}$$\end{document}sinv0(t)2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cos \tfrac{v_0(t)}{2}$$\end{document}cosv0(t)2 by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sin \tfrac{v_0(t)}{2}=\beta (t),\qquad \cos \tfrac{v_0(t)}{2}=\alpha (t). $$\end{document}sinv0(t)2=β(t),cosv0(t)2=α(t).Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sin v_0(t)=2\alpha (t)\beta (t),\qquad \cos v_0(t)=\alpha ^2(t)-\beta ^2(t). $$\end{document}sinv0(t)=2α(t)β(t),cosv0(t)=α2(t)-β2(t).and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ v_{0t}=\cos v_0 (\sin v_0)_t-\sin v_0(\cos v_0)_t=-2\alpha \beta (\alpha +\beta )(\alpha _t+\beta _t). $$\end{document}v0t=cosv0(sinv0)t-sinv0(cosv0)t=-2αβ(α+β)(αt+βt).

The inverse t-spectral mapping

Naturally, Problems I and II share the same t-spectral mapping.

The master Riemann–Hilbert problems

Proposition 5.1

There exists a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{(xt)}(x,t,k)$$\end{document}M(xt)(x,t,k) of the RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(xt)}$$\end{document}RH(xt) for all x and t. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det M^{(xt)}\equiv 1$$\end{document}detM(xt)≡1 and5.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M^{(xt)}( k)=\overline{ M^{(xt)}(-\bar{k})},\qquad M^{(xt)}(k)=\sigma _2\overline{ M^{(xt)}(\bar{k})}\sigma _2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(xt)(k)=M(xt)(-k¯)¯,M(xt)(k)=σ2M(xt)(k¯)¯σ2

Concerning the proof, notice that the jump matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ J^{(xt)}(x,t, k)$$\end{document}J(xt)(x,t,k) defined in (5.2b)–(5.2c) is positively defined on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R and satisfies the symmetry\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} J^{(xt)}(x, t,k)=\overline{ J^{(xt)T}(x,t,\bar{k})}, \qquad k\in \{|k|=\epsilon \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J(xt)(x,t,k)=J(xt)T(x,t,k¯)¯,k∈{|k|=ϵ}.These properties allow us to invoke Zhou’s vanishing lemma (see [21]), which ensures the solvability of the Riemann–Hilbert problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(xt)}$$\end{document}RH(xt).

Remark 5.2

The construction of the master RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(xt)}$$\end{document}RH(xt) uses the same data as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(x)}$$\end{document}RH(x), i.e., the scattering functions associated with the initial condition alone.

Recovering u(x, t) from the solution of the RH problem

In this subsection, we describe the derivation of a (local) solution to the sG equation (together with the derivation of the corresponding Lax pair equations), starting from a RH problem parametrized by x and t, motivated by the considerations in Sect. 5.

Let Γ Γ denote an oriented contour in the complex plane, invariant under the symmetries k↦ -kk↦-k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\mapsto \bar{k}$$\end{document}k↦k¯. Proposition 6.5

Introducing u(x, t) up to 2π k2πk such that6.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sin \frac{u}{2} = \beta , \qquad \cos \frac{u}{2} =\alpha \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sinu2=β,cosu2=αor6.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sin \frac{u}{2} = -\beta , \qquad \cos \frac{u}{2} =-\alpha \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sinu2=-β,cosu2=-αequations (6.25a)– (6.25c) reduce to (1.1).

Proof

First notice that (6.26) implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sin u=2\alpha \beta ,\qquad \cos u=\alpha ^2-\beta ^2. $$\end{document}sinu=2αβ,cosu=α2-β2.Moreover, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^2+\beta ^2=1$$\end{document}α2+β2=1, α α and β β cannot be simultaneously 0.

Combining these facts with (6.25c) (in case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^2-\beta ^2\ne 0$$\end{document}α2-β2≠0) or (6.25b) (in case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\alpha \beta \ne 0$$\end{document}2αβ≠0), we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \xi =u_x. $$\end{document}ξ=ux.Consequently, (6.25a) implies (1.1). □ □

Differentiability of solution of master RH problems

Basic facts about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-RH problems

For our purposes, we will need several results concerning the equivalence between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 Riemann–Hilbert problems (whose jump matrices may be discontinuous at intersection points of the contour) and singular integral equations; see, for example, [22, 23]. We collect the necessary definitions and results below.

Throughout this section, Γ Γ denotes an oriented contour consisting of a finite union of piece-wise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 arcs. The arcs may intersect only at their endpoints, and all intersections are assumed to be transversal. In particular, this class of contours includes configurations such as the union of the real line with finitely many circles.

The orientation of Γ Γ is chosen so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Gamma =\partial D_+=-\partial D_-, $$\end{document}Γ=∂D+=-∂D-,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}\setminus \Gamma =D_+\cup D_-$$\end{document}C\Γ=D+∪D-.

For a bounded component D of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Problem I

We assume that a(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯. Proposition 7.3

The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu ^{(xt)}(x,t,k)$$\end{document}μ(xt)(x,t,k) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 with respect to x and t.

Proof

Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \rightarrow L^2(\Sigma ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(x,t)↦w^±(x,t):(R+,[0,T))→L2(Σ);Q:(w^+,w^-)↦Cw^I:L2(Σ)×L2(Σ)→L2(Σ)and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}kp(x,t,k)}$$\end{document}eikp(x,t,k) we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} On the other hand, these properties together with the dominated convergence theorem yield the differentiability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\mapsto \hat{w}^\pm (x,t):(\mathbb {R}^+,[0,T))\rightarrow L^2(\Sigma )$$\end{document}(x,t)↦w^±(x,t):(R+,[0,T))→L2(Σ).

The maps F and Q are linear, and estimates (7.3a) and (7.3b) imply their differentiability. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dF:(u^+,u^-)\mapsto -C_u:L^\infty (\Sigma )\times L^\infty (\Sigma )\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(L^2(\Sigma ))$$\end{document}dF:(u+,u-)↦-Cu:L∞(Σ)×L∞(Σ)→B(L2(Σ)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dQ:(u^+,u^-)\mapsto C_u I:L^2(\Sigma )\times L^2(\Sigma )\rightarrow L^2(\Sigma )$$\end{document}dQ:(u+,u-)↦CuI:L2(Σ)×L2(Σ)→L2(Σ). Hence, by the chain rule (see [25]), the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\mapsto (1\!\!1-C_{\hat{w}(x,t)})^{-1}:(\mathbb {R}^+,[0,T))\rightarrow \mathcal {B}(L^2(\Sigma ))$$\end{document}(x,t)↦(1-Cw^(x,t))-1:(R+,[0,T))→B(L2(Σ)) is differentiable together with its inverse. The chain rule again implies differentiability of the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\mapsto C_{\hat{w}(x,t)}I: (\mathbb {R}^+,[0,T))\rightarrow L^2(\Sigma )$$\end{document}(x,t)↦Cw^(x,t)I:(R+,[0,T))→L2(Σ). Consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mu }^{(xt)}(x,t,k)$$\end{document}μ^(xt)(x,t,k) is differentiable as the composition of differentiable maps. □ □

Notice that properties (7.15) and (7.16) imply that the first term in the expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{M}(x,t,k)$$\end{document}M^(x,t,k) as k → 0k→0, as well as the second term in the expansion as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, coincide with those of M(x, t, k). Thus, Proposition 7.3 together with the dominated convergence theorem implies the following corollary:

Corollary 7.4

The functions α (x,t)α(x,t), β (x,t)β(x,t) and ξ (x,t)ξ(x,t) are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 with respect to x and t.

Problem II

The idea of the proof that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\check{M}^{(xt)}(x,t,k)$$\end{document}Mˇ(xt)(x,t,k) is differentiable with respect to x and t is essentially the same as in the case of Problem I. Therefore, we only outline the necessary modifications.

In the case of Problem II, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^*(k)$$\end{document}d∗(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯: Zeros in the open upper half-plane can be eliminated by introducing small circular contours and converting the residue conditions into jump conditions, zeros on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R can be eliminated by introducing small half-circular contours.

The uniqueness and existence results for Problem I

Uniqueness result for Problem I

In this section, assuming the existence of a solution to Problem I (i.e., with an appropriate behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞), we address its uniqueness.

Theorem 8.1

Let u(x, t) be a solution of the sG equation in the domain x>0x>0, 0<t<T0<t<T such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in C^1([0,T],\tilde{H}^{1,2}((0,\infty )))$$\end{document}u∈C1([0,T],H~1,2((0,∞))). Then, u(x, t) is uniquely determined by u(x, 0), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [0,\infty )$$\end{document}x∈[0,∞), through a unique solution of the Riemann–Hilbert problem, for which the data (jump matrix and residue condition) are given in terms of the spectral functions associated with u(x, 0).

Remark 8.2

In general, the spectral functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^{(j)*}(k)$$\end{document}b(j)∗(k) are determined for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in {\mathbb {C}}^-$$\end{document}k∈C-, but their combination as in the l.h.s. of (8.10) turns out to be analytically continued into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}^+$$\end{document}C+.

Proof

In view of the construction of RH problems and the way of extracting the solution of the sG equation from the solution of the associated RH problem, it is sufficient to show that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in {\mathbb {C}}^+$$\end{document}k∈C+ and for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\ge l_1$$\end{document}x≥l1, t≥ 0t≥0, (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)} = O(1/k) $$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k)=O(1/k) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞;(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)} = O(k) $$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k)=O(k) as k→ 0k→0.Regarding item (i), notice that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in {\mathbb {C}}^+\cap \{|k|>\epsilon \}$$\end{document}k∈C+∩{|k|>ϵ},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)} = \frac{e^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)}}{a^{(1)}(k)a^{(2)}(k)} \\ & \quad \left( b^{(1)*}(k)a^{(2)}(k) - b^{(2)*}(k)a^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k\mu }\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k)=e2ikp1(x,t,k)a(1)(k)a(2)(k)b(1)∗(k)a(2)(k)-b(2)∗(k)a(1)(k)e2ikμand thus, taking into account (8.10), the analyticity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{(j)}(k)$$\end{document}a(j)(k) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}^+$$\end{document}C+ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{(j)}(k)=1+O\left( \frac{1}{k}\right) $$\end{document}a(j)(k)=1+O1k, and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Im}p_1(x,t,k)\ge 0$$\end{document}Imp1(x,t,k)≥0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\ge l_1$$\end{document}x≥l1, t≥ 0t≥0, the statement of (i) follows.

(i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)} = O(1/k) $$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k)=O(1/k) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞;

Regarding item (ii), we notice that the estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)}=O(k)$$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k)=O(k) follow from (2.42), the estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{a}^{(j)}(k) = 1 + O(k)$$\end{document}a~(j)(k)=1+O(k) as k→ 0k→0, and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{(1)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_1(x,t,k)} - X^{(2)}(k)\textrm{e}^{2\textrm{i}k p_2(x,t,k)}$$\end{document}X(1)(k)e2ikp1(x,t,k)-X(2)(k)e2ikp2(x,t,k) is analytic in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}^+$$\end{document}C+ including near k=0k=0 (see Remark 8.2).

□ □

Behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞

In Sects. 8.1 and 8.2, we assumed the existence of a solution to Problem I for the sine-Gordon equation (with prescribed behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞) and constructed the associated Riemann–Hilbert problem from the corresponding Jost solutions. In the present and the following section, we drop this assumption and instead analyze the Riemann–Hilbert problem constructed from initial data belonging to a suitable class. The differentiability of the ingredients extracted from this Riemann–Hilbert problem, which are used in the reconstruction of the solution to the sine-Gordon equation, was established in Sect. 7.2. In this section, we focus on the behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞. To fix ideas, we assume that a(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯..

First, notice that the considerations in Sect. 8.2 imply that extending the initial data to the left does not affect the solution of the problem on the common half-line. In particular, one may extend the domain (0,+∞ )(0,+∞) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-\infty ,+\infty )$$\end{document}(-∞,+∞), thus reducing Problem I to a problem on the whole real line. Consequently, without loss of generality, we can assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _2 = 0$$\end{document}κ2=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(k)=O\left( \frac{1}{k^2}\right) $$\end{document}r(k)=O1k2 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞ (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(k), ~kr(k) \in L^{2}((0,\infty ))$$\end{document}r(k),kr(k)∈L2((0,∞))), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(k) = O(k^3)$$\end{document}r(k)=O(k3) as k → 0k→0. To ensure the last condition, we extend the initial function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(x)$$\end{document}u0(x) from (0,+∞ )(0,+∞) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-\infty ,+\infty )$$\end{document}(-∞,+∞) in such a way that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{-\infty }^{\infty } \sin u_0(y)\textrm{d}y=0$$\end{document}∫-∞∞sinu0(y)dy=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{-\infty }^{\infty } y\sin u_0(y)\textrm{d}y=0$$\end{document}∫-∞∞ysinu0(y)dy=0.

The dominated convergence theorem, together with the properties of r(k), implies that we can pass to the (non-tangential) limits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞ and k→ 0k→0 in (7.13). In particular, we have8.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \xi (x,t)=&\frac{2}{\pi }\int _{\mathbb {R}} \mu _{11}^{(xt)}(x,t,z) r^*(z)\textrm{e}^{-2\textrm{i}z x+\frac{\textrm{i}t}{2z}} \textrm{d}z, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ξ(x,t)=2π∫Rμ11(xt)(x,t,z)r∗(z)e-2izx+it2zdz,8.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \beta (x,t)=&\frac{1}{2\pi \textrm{i}}\int _{\mathbb {R}} \mu _{11}^{(xt)}(x,t,z) \frac{r^*(z)}{z}\textrm{e}^{-2\textrm{i}z x+\frac{\textrm{i}t}{2z}} \textrm{d}z. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β(x,t)=12πi∫Rμ11(xt)(x,t,z)r∗(z)ze-2izx+it2zdz.Notice that the following analogs of Lemma 2.3 and Theorem 2.1 from [26] hold:

Lemma 8.5

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(k)\in H^n(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}r(k)∈Hn(R) and r(k) vanishes to order at least 2n at k=0k=0. Proof

First, consider (8.13).

Applying Lemma 8.4 and Lemma 8.5 in the case n=0n=0, we obtain the following

Therefore, applying Lemma 8.4 and Lemma 8.5 in the case n=1n=1, we have

Existence result for Problem I

Theorem 8.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(x)\in H^{2,2}((0,+\infty ))+2\pi k$$\end{document}u0(x)∈H2,2((0,+∞))+2πk with some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z and let a(k) be the spectral function associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 through the direct x-spectral problem. Assume that a(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯.

Then the problem 8.22a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&u_{xt} = \sin u,\quad \quad x \ge 0,\quad 0\le t< T<\infty ;\end{aligned}$$\end{document}uxt=sinu,x≥0,0≤t<T<∞;8.22b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&u(x,0) = u_0(x), \quad x \ge 0;\end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(x,0)=u0(x),x≥0;8.22c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&u(\cdot ,t)\in \tilde{H}^{1,2}((0,+\infty )), \quad 0\le t< T<\infty \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(·,t)∈H~1,2((0,+∞)),0≤t<T<∞ has a unique solution, u(x, t), which can be represented in terms of the solution of the associated RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(xt)}$$\end{document}RH(xt) via (8.2)–(8.3).

Proof

As it was shown above, the existence of a solution of the RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(xt)}$$\end{document}RH(xt) follows from Zhou’s vanishing lemma. Next, we construct u(x, t) by following Steps 2–4 of Proposition 8.1. As shown in Sect. 6, the function u(x, t) constructed in this way indeed satisfies the sG equation. Moreover, the considerations of Sect. 7.2 imply that u(x, t) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 with respect to both variables x and t, whereas Sect. 8.3 establishes that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(x,t)\in \tilde{H}^{1,2}((0,+\infty ))$$\end{document}u(x,t)∈H~1,2((0,+∞)).

Therefore, it remains to prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(x,0)=u_0(x)$$\end{document}u(x,0)=u0(x) for all x≥ 0x≥0. Notice that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${ M}^{(xt)}(x,0,k)$$\end{document}M(xt)(x,0,k) satisfies the RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(x)}$$\end{document}RH(x), see Sect. 4.1.1. Thus by uniqueness of solution of RH problem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {RH}}^{(x)}$$\end{document}RH(x), we conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{ M}^{(xt)}(y,0,k)=\hat{ M}^{(x)}(y,k)$$\end{document}M^(xt)(y,0,k)=M^(x)(y,k). In particular,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {u_x}(x,0)={u}_{0x}(x). $$\end{document}ux(x,0)=u0x(x).From the solution of the Riemann–Hilbert problem, we extract the quantities \sin u(x,t)sinu(x,t), \cos u(x,t)cosu(x,t), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_x(x,t)$$\end{document}ux(x,t). Hence, the function u(x, t) can be recovered up to an additive constant of the form 2π k2πk, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z. The constant is then uniquely determined by imposing the initial condition, together with continuity of the reconstructed solution. □ □

Remark 8.9

The assumption that a(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯ is made in order to focus on the main ideas and avoid additional technical details. Zeros in the open upper half-plane can be handled by introducing small circular contours and converting the corresponding residue conditions into jump conditions, while zeros on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R can be treated by introducing small half-circular contours.

The uniqueness and existence results for Problem II

Uniqueness result for Problem II

Similarly to Problem I, in this section, assuming the existence of a solution to Problem II (with prescribed behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}x→+∞), we address its uniqueness.

Behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}x→-∞

In Sect. 9.1, the Riemann–Hilbert problem was derived under the assumption that a solution to Problem II for the sine-Gordon equation exists and satisfies prescribed asymptotic conditions as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}x→-∞. We now reverse this perspective: Instead of starting from a solution of the sine-Gordon equation, we take initial data from an appropriate class and study the corresponding Riemann–Hilbert problem directly. The regularity properties of the quantities involved in the reconstruction procedure were established in Sect. 7.3. The aim of the present section is to analyze the behavior of the reconstructed solution as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}x→-∞. To fix ideas, we assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^*(k)$$\end{document}d∗(k) has no zeros in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {C}^+}$$\end{document}C+¯.

First, we construct h and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{h}$$\end{document}h~ to improve the behavior at k=∞ k=∞ and k=0k=0, and to achieve the matching near k=ε k=ϵ (c.f. Existence result for Problem II

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u_0(x)\in H^{2,2}((-\infty ,0))+2\pi k; ~v_0(t)\in H^{1}(0, T)\}$$\end{document}{u0(x)∈H2,2((-∞,0))+2πk;v0(t)∈H1(0,T)} be a set of functions satisfying internal compatibility condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(0)=v_0(0)$$\end{document}u0(0)=v0(0). Furthermore, Sect. 6 shows that u(x, t) satisfies the sG equation, while Sect. 7.3 implies that u(x, t) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^1$$\end{document}C1 in both x and t, and Sect. 9.2 establishes that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(x,t)\in \tilde{H}^{1,2}((-\infty ,0))$$\end{document}u(x,t)∈H~1,2((-∞,0)).

□ □

Remark 9.4

The above zero-free assumptions are made in order to focus on the main ideas and avoid additional technical details; cf. Remark 8.9.

Concluding remarks

Throughout this paper, we consider the generic case, in the sense that the initial data for Problem I and the initial and boundary data for Problem II are assumed to generate the corresponding spectral functions with finitely many zeros, all of which are simple. Under this assumption, the associated RH problems can be formulated by imposing residue conditions at the zeros of the spectral functions. The restriction to simple zeros is not essential, however. Indeed, the general case can be treated by replacing the residue conditions with a jump condition on a sufficiently large circle enclosing all the zeros; see [9, 11, 27].
